Hard and Soft Consonants

In Russian, most consonants can be hard or soft. This changes meaning. For example, 'Ð¼Ð°Ñ' (checkmate) vs 'Ð¼Ð°ÑÑ' (mother). The soft sign 'Ñ' makes a consonant soft. Vowel Reduction and Stress

Russian vowels change sound when unstressed. This is called reduction. Stressed vowels are clear and long. Unstressed vowels become shorter and weaker. For example, 'Ð¾' becomes 'a' when unstressed: 'ÐÐ¾ÑÐºÐ²Ð°' sounds like 'Moskva' but the 'o' is 'a'â'Maskva'. 'Ðµ' becomes 'i' when unstressed: 'ÑÐµÐ»ÐµÑÐ¾Ð½' sounds like 'tilifon'. 'Ñ' becomes 'i' or 'e': 'ÑÐ·ÑÐº' sounds like 'yizyk'. Stress is unpredictableâyou must learn each word's stress. In dictionaries, stress is marked with an accent: 'ÑÐ¾ÑÐ¾ÑÐ¾Ì'. 'ÐÐ¾ ÑÐ²Ð¸Ð´Ð°Ð½Ð¸Ñ' (do svidaniya) â stress on 'Ð°', so 'o' reduces. Listen to native speakers and imitate. Use Forvo.com to hear words. Record yourself. Compare. Spend 15 minutes daily. In Africa, many languages have vowel harmony or tone, but Russian stress is different. It's like English stress but more important. Wrong stress can confuse. For example, 'Ð·Ð°ÌÐ¼Ð¾Ðº' (castle) vs 'Ð·Ð°Ð¼Ð¾ÌÐº' (lock). Practice reading aloud: 'Ð¯ Ð»ÑÐ±Ð»ÑÌ ÐÐ¾ÑÐºÐ²ÑÌ' (I love Moscow). 'ÐÐ½Ð°Ì Ð¶Ð¸Ð²ÑÑ Ð² ÐÐ¸ÌÐµÐ²Ðµ' (She lives in Kiev). Write sentences. Underline stressed vowels. Say them. After a week, you will reduce vowels naturally. Next, reading practice..Trim()

Module 2 · Basic Greetings and Introductions

Hello, Goodbye, and Polite Expressions

In Russian, the most common greeting is "ÐÐ´ÑÐ°Ð²ÑÑÐ²ÑÐ¹ÑÐµ" (Zdravstvuyte) for formal situations and "ÐÑÐ¸Ð²ÐµÑ" (Privet) for friends. In African contexts, where respect for elders is paramount, always use the formal "ÐÐ´ÑÐ°Ð²ÑÑÐ²ÑÐ¹ÑÐµ" with older people, officials, or strangers. For example, when greeting a market vendor in Lagos or Nairobi, start with "ÐÐ´ÑÐ°Ð²ÑÑÐ²ÑÐ¹ÑÐµ" and a smile. Russians appreciate effort, so even a simple "ÐÑÐ¸Ð²ÐµÑ" among peers can break the ice. Practice these phrases daily: greet your family in Russian in the morning, say "Ð¡Ð¿Ð°ÑÐ¸Ð±Ð¾" when served tea. Use flashcards or apps like Anki to memorize. Record yourself and compare with native speakers on YouTube. Remember, tone matters: "ÐÑÐ¸Ð²ÐµÑ" is friendly, "ÐÐ´ÑÐ°Ð²ÑÑÐ²ÑÐ¹ÑÐµ" is respectful. In business settings, always use formal greetings until invited to switch. This lesson gives you the foundation to start any conversation politely..Trim()

They answer: "ÐÐµÐ½Ñ Ð·Ð¾Ð²ÑÑ ÐÐ²Ð°Ð½. Ð ÐÐ°Ñ?" You: "ÐÐµÐ½Ñ Ð·Ð¾Ð²ÑÑ Ðofi. ÐÑÐºÑÐ´Ð° ÐÑ?" They: "Ð¯ Ð¸Ð· ÐÐ¾ÑÐºÐ²Ñ. Ð ÐÑ?" You: "Ð¯ Ð¸Ð· ÐÐºÐºÑÑ." This exchange is natural and respectful. Use apps like HelloTalk to practice with native speakers. Listen to Russian podcasts for intonation. Write down the questions and answers in a notebook. Repeat aloud until fluent. This skill is crucial for networking and building relationships..Trim()

Formal vs. Informal Address (ÑÑ vs. Ð²Ñ)

Russian has two forms of "you": "ÑÑ" (ty) informal singular and "Ð²Ñ" (vy) formal or plural. Use "Ð²Ñ" with strangers, elders, officials, and in professional settings. Use "ÑÑ" with friends, family, children, and peers after mutual agreement. In African cultures, respect for elders is key, so always start with "Ð²Ñ" unless invited to use "ÑÑ". For example, to a professor: "ÐÐ°Ðº Ð²Ñ Ð¿Ð¾Ð¶Ð¸Ð²Ð°ÐµÑÐµ?" (Kak vy pozhivayete?) â How are you? To a friend: "ÐÐ°Ðº ÑÑ?" (Kak ty?) â How are you? The verb endings change: with "Ð²Ñ", use plural verb forms; with "ÑÑ", use singular. For instance, "ÐÑ Ð³Ð¾Ð²Ð¾ÑÐ¸ÑÐµ Ð¿Ð¾-ÑÑÑÑÐºÐ¸?" (Vy govorite po-russki?) â Do you speak Russian? vs. "Ð¢Ñ Ð³Ð¾Ð²Ð¾ÑÐ¸ÑÑ Ð¿Ð¾-ÑÑÑÑÐºÐ¸?" (Ty govorish po-russki?). To switch from "Ð²Ñ" to "ÑÑ", you might say "ÐÐ°Ð²Ð°Ð¹ÑÐµ Ð½Ð° Â«ÑÑÂ»?" (Davayte na "ty") â Let's use "ÑÑ". This is common among young people or after getting to know someone. In business, never switch without permission. In African business meetings, using "Ð²Ñ" shows professionalism. Practice with dialogues: A boss says to employee: "ÐÐ²Ð°Ð½, ÐºÐ°Ðº Ð²Ñ ÑÐµÐ±Ñ ÑÑÐ²ÑÑÐ²ÑÐµÑÐµ?" Employee: "Ð¡Ð¿Ð°ÑÐ¸Ð±Ð¾, ÑÐ¾ÑÐ¾ÑÐ¾. Ð Ð²Ñ?" Then later, if they become friends, boss might say: "ÐÐ°Ð²Ð°Ð¹ Ð½Ð° Â«ÑÑÂ»." Use online exercises to drill verb conjugations. Watch YouTube videos on Russian etiquette. Remember, using "ÑÑ" incorrectly can offend. When in doubt, use "Ð²Ñ". This lesson helps you navigate social hierarchies respectfully..Trim()

Basic Professions and Occupations

To talk about professions in Russian, use "Ð¯ ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÐ°Ñ..." (Ya rabotayu...) meaning "I work as..." or "Ð¯..." (Ya...) plus the profession. Common professions: Ð²ÑÐ°Ñ (vrach) â doctor, ÑÑÐ¸ÑÐµÐ»Ñ (uchitel) â teacher, Ð¸Ð½Ð¶ÐµÐ½ÐµÑ (inzhener) â engineer, Ð¿ÑÐ¾Ð³ÑÐ°Ð¼Ð¼Ð¸ÑÑ (programmist) â programmer, Ð±Ð¸Ð·Ð½ÐµÑÐ¼ÐµÐ½ (biznesmen) â businessman, Ð¿ÑÐ¾Ð´Ð°Ð²ÐµÑ (prodavets) â salesperson, Ð²Ð¾Ð´Ð¸ÑÐµÐ»Ñ (voditel) â driver, Ð¿Ð¾Ð²Ð°Ñ (povar) â cook, Ð¼ÐµÐ´ÑÐµÑÑÑÐ° (medsestra) â nurse, ÑÑÑÐ´ÐµÐ½Ñ (student) â student. "Ð¯ ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÐ°Ñ Ð² ÑÐºÐ¾Ð»Ðµ" (Ya rabotayu v shkole) â I work at a school. To ask "Where do you work?" say "ÐÐ´Ðµ ÐÑ ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÐ°ÐµÑÐµ?" (Gde Vy rabotayete?) formally. Module 3 · Numbers, Time, and Dates

Numbers 0-20

In Russian, numbers 0-20 are the foundation for telling time, dates, and prices. Start with 0-10: nol (0), odin (1), dva (2), tri (3), chetyre (4), pyat (5), shest (6), sem (7), vosem (8), devyat (9), desyat (10). Practice saying your phone number or the price of a kilo of tomatoes at your local market. For example, if a kilo of onions costs 15 rubles, you say 'pyatnadtsat rubley'. Note that 'odin' changes to 'odna' for feminine nouns (e.g., odna minuta) and 'odno' for neuter (e.g., odno yabloko)..Trim()

.Trim()

From 11 to 19, the pattern is number + 'nadtsat': odinnadtsat (11), dvenadtsat (12), trinadtsat (13), chetyrnadtsat (14), pyatnadtsat (15), shestnadtsat (16), semnadtsat (17), vosemnadtsat (18), devyatnadtsat (19). For 20, say 'dvadtsat'. To ask 'How much?' in Russian, say 'Skolko stoit?' (How much does it cost?). When bargaining in a market in Nairobi or Lagos, knowing numbers helps you avoid overpaying. For example, if a vendor says 'sto rubley' (100 rubles), you can respond with 'dvadtsat' (20) as a counteroffer..Trim()

.Trim()

Practice counting aloud daily: count steps, cars, or coins. Use a language app like Duolingo or Memrise to drill numbers. Write numbers in a notebook and say them in Russian. For listening practice, search YouTube for 'Russian numbers 0-20 pronunciation'. Remember that stress can change meaning: 'sorok' (40) vs 'soroka' (magpie). But for 0-20, stress is usually on the last syllable for 11-19, and on the first for 0-10. With consistent practice, you'll master these numbers in a week..Trim()

Numbers 21-100 and Beyond

Once you know 0-20, building numbers 21-100 is straightforward. For 21-29, combine 'dvadtsat' (20) with the unit: dvadtsat odin (21), dvadtsat dva (22), etc. For 30, say 'tridtsat'; 40 is 'sorok'; 50 is 'pyatdesyat'; 60 is 'shestdesyat'; 70 is 'semdesyat'; 80 is 'vosemdesyat'; 90 is 'devyanosto'; 100 is 'sto'. Notice that 40 ('sorok') is irregularâit doesn't follow the pattern of other tens. Memorize it early..Trim()

.Trim()

For numbers like 35, say 'tridtsat pyat' (30 and 5). For 99, say 'devyanosto devyat'. When you reach 100, you can combine: sto odin (101), sto dvadtsat tri (123). For hundreds, use 'dvesti' (200), 'trista' (300), 'chetyresta' (400), 'pyatsot' (500), etc. Thousands: 'tysyacha' (1000), 'dve tysyachi' (2000). For example, 'dve tysyachi devyatnadtsat' is 2019..Trim()

.Trim()

In practical situations like buying a bus ticket from Kigali to Kampala, you might hear 'sorok tysyach' (40,000). Practice by reading prices aloud: a bag of maize flour might cost 'shestdesyat pyat rubley' (65 rubles). Use a Russian number chart or flashcards. For listening, search YouTube for 'Russian numbers 1-100'. Try to count backwards from 100 to 0. Write random numbers and say them in Russian. With daily practice, you'll handle any number up to 1000 within two weeks..Trim()

Telling Time: Hours and Minutes

Telling time in Russian uses the 24-hour clock in formal situations, but the 12-hour clock is common in speech. To ask 'What time is it?', say 'Kotoryy chas?' (literally 'Which hour?'). The answer uses 'chas' (hour) for 1, 'chasa' for 2-4, and 'chasov' for 5-20. For minutes, use 'minuta' (1), 'minuty' (2-4), 'minut' (5-20). For example, 3:05 is 'tri chasa pyat minut' (three hours five minutes). For 'past' and 'to', use 'posle' (after) and 'do' (before). So 3:15 is 'pyatnadtsat minut posle trekh' (15 minutes after three)..Trim()

.Trim()

In everyday conversation, you might hear 'polovina' for half past: 'polovina chetvertogo' is 3:30 (half of the fourth hour). For 3:45, say 'bez pyatnadtsati chetyre' (without 15, four). To say 'at' a time, use 'v': 'v tri chasa' (at 3 o'clock). For example, 'My vstrechaemsya v shest chasov vechera' (We meet at 6 PM). Note that 'utra' (morning), 'dnya' (afternoon), 'vechera' (evening), and 'nochi' (night) can be added: 'v sem chasov utra' (at 7 AM)..Trim()

.Trim()

Practice by setting your phone to Russian time format and reading the time aloud every hour. Use a clock face and say the time in Russian. For listening, search YouTube for 'Russian telling time'. When scheduling a meeting with a Russian speaker, confirm: 'Vo skolko?' (At what time?). If you're in Lagos and need to call Moscow, remember the time differenceâMoscow is 2 hours ahead of Lagos in winter. Always double-check times to avoid confusion..Trim()

Days of the Week and Months

The Russian week starts on Monday: ponedelnik (Monday), vtornik (Tuesday), sreda (Wednesday), chetverg (Thursday), pyatnitsa (Friday), subbota (Saturday), voskresenye (Sunday). Notice that 'voskresenye' also means 'resurrection'. To say 'on Monday', use 'v ponedelnik'. For 'on Mondays' (repeated), say 'po ponedelnikam'. Practice by writing your weekly schedule in Russian: 'V ponedelnik ya rabotayu' (On Monday I work)..Trim()

.Trim()

The months are: yanvar (January), fevral (February), mart (March), aprel (April), may (May), iyun (June), iyul (July), avgust (August), sentyabr (September), oktyabr (October), noyabr (November), dekabr (December). To say 'in January', use 'v yanvare'. For example, 'Moy den rozhdeniya v marte' (My birthday is in March). Note that months are not capitalized in Russian unless at the start of a sentence..Trim()

.Trim()

In practical contexts, you might need to say the day and date: 'Segodnya ponedelnik, pyatoye maya' (Today is Monday, the fifth of May). To ask 'What day is it today?', say 'Kakoy segodnya den?' For 'What month is it?', say 'Kakoy segodnya mesyats?' Practice by keeping a diary in Russian, writing the day and month each morning. Use a calendar with Russian names. For listening, search YouTube for 'Russian days of the week song'. Knowing days and months helps you make appointments and understand schedules, whether in Nairobi or Moscow..Trim()

Dates and Ordinal Numbers

In Russian, dates use ordinal numbers (first, second, third) for the day, and the month is in the genitive case. For 'the first of May', say 'pervoye maya'. The ordinal numbers for days 1-31 are: pervyy (1st), vtoroy (2nd), tretiy (3rd), chetvertyy (4th), pyatyy (5th), shestoy (6th), sedmoy (7th), vosmoy (8th), devyatyy (9th), desyatyy (10th), odinnadtsatyy (11th), dvenadtsatyy (12th), trinadtsatyy (13th), chetyrnadtsatyy (14th), pyatnadtsatyy (15th), shestnadtsatyy (16th), semnadtsatyy (17th), vosemnadtsatyy (18th), devyatnadtsatyy (19th), dvadtsatyy (20th), dvadtsat pervyy (21st), etc. For 30th: tridtsatyy; 31st: tridtsat pervyy..Trim()

.Trim()

When writing a date, Russians use day.month.year: 05.05.2025. To say 'on the fifth of May', say 'pyatogo maya'. The ending '-ogo' is used for dates. For example, 'Ya rodilsya pyatogo maya' (I was born on the fifth of May). To ask 'What's the date today?', say 'Kakoye segodnya chislo?' The answer: 'Segodnya pyatoye maya' (Today is the fifth of May). Note that 'chislo' means 'date' or 'number'..Trim()

.Trim()

Practice by writing today's date in Russian each morning. Use a calendar and say the date aloud. For listening, search YouTube for 'Russian dates and ordinals'. When filling out forms in Russian, you'll need to write the date in the correct format. For example, a visa application might ask for 'data rozhdeniya' (date of birth). Knowing ordinals also helps with floors: 'pervyy etazh' (first floor). With practice, you'll master dates in a week..Trim()

Practical: Making Appointments and Schedules

Making appointments in Russian requires combining numbers, time, and dates. Start with a polite request: 'Mozhno s vami vstrechatsya?' (Can I meet with you?). Suggest a time: 'Davayte vstrechaemsya v sreda v tri chasa' (Let's meet on Wednesday at 3 o'clock). To confirm, say 'Vam udobno?' (Is it convenient for you?). If you need to reschedule, say 'Izvinite, mozhno perenesti?' (Sorry, can we reschedule?). Use 'v' for days and times: 'v ponedelnik v desyat chasov utra' (on Monday at 10 AM)..Trim()

.Trim()

For scheduling, you might say: 'U menya vstrecha v chetverg v chetyre chasa' (I have a meeting on Thursday at 4 o'clock). To ask about someone's schedule, say 'Kogda vy svobodny?' (When are you free?). If you're busy, say 'Ya zanyat' (I'm busy). For a phone call, say 'Ya pozvonyu vam zavtra v sem chasov vechera' (I'll call you tomorrow at 7 PM). Remember to use the 24-hour clock in formal writing: 'v 15:00' (at 15:00)..Trim()

.Trim()

Practice by role-playing with a friend: one person suggests a time, the other accepts or declines. Use a planner and write your appointments in Russian. For example, 'Vtornik, 10:00 - vstrecha s klientom' (Tuesday, 10:00 - meeting with client). For listening, search YouTube for 'Russian making appointments'. In African business contexts, punctuality is valued, so always confirm times. If you're in Accra and scheduling with a partner in Moscow, note the time difference (Moscow is 3 hours ahead of Accra in winter). Double-check dates to avoid mistakes. With practice, you'll handle appointments confidently..Trim()

Module 4 · Everyday Objects and Gender of Nouns

Masculine, Feminine, and Neuter Nouns

Russian nouns have three genders: masculine, feminine, and neuter. The gender is usually shown by the ending of the word in its dictionary form. Masculine nouns typically end in a consonant, like ÑÑÐ¾Ð» (stol, table), ÑÑÑÐ» (stul, chair), Ð½Ð¾Ð¶ (nozh, knife). They can also end in -Ð¹, like ÑÐ°Ð¹ (chay, tea), or -Ñ, like ÑÐ»Ð¾Ð²Ð°ÑÑ (slovar', dictionary). Feminine nouns often end in -Ð° or -Ñ, like ÑÐ°ÑÐºÐ° (chashka, cup), Ð»Ð°Ð¼Ð¿Ð° (lampa, lamp), Ð½ÐµÐ´ÐµÐ»Ñ (nedelya, week). They can also end in -Ñ, like Ð´Ð²ÐµÑÑ (dver', door), ÐºÑÐ¾Ð²Ð°ÑÑ (krovat', bed). Neuter nouns typically end in -Ð¾ or -Ðµ, like Ð¾ÐºÐ½Ð¾ (okno, window), Ð¼ÑÐ»Ð¾ (mylo, soap), Ð¿Ð¾Ð»Ð¾ÑÐµÐ½ÑÐµ (polotentse, towel). Knowing gender is crucial because adjectives and possessive pronouns must agree with the noun's gender. For example, 'my table' is Ð¼Ð¾Ð¹ ÑÑÐ¾Ð» (moy stol) masculine, 'my cup' is Ð¼Ð¾Ñ ÑÐ°ÑÐºÐ° (moya chashka) feminine, 'my window' is Ð¼Ð¾Ñ Ð¾ÐºÐ½Ð¾ (moyo okno) neuter. In African languages, noun classes are common, so this concept may be familiar. To practice, take 10 household items and sort them into three columns by gender. Use a dictionary if unsure. Remember that words ending in -Ñ can be either masculine or feminine; you must memorize them. For instance, Ð´ÐµÐ½Ñ (den', day) is masculine, but Ð½Ð¾ÑÑ (noch', night) is feminine. Make flashcards with the word on one side and its gender on the other. Test yourself daily. Soon, you will instinctively know the gender of new words by their endings. This will make your Russian more accurate and natural..Trim()

Plural Forms of Nouns

Forming plurals in Russian depends on the gender and the ending of the noun. For masculine nouns ending in a consonant, add -Ñ: ÑÑÐ¾Ð» (stol) becomes ÑÑÐ¾Ð»Ñ (stoly, tables). If the stem ends in Ðº, Ð³, Ñ, Ð¶, Ñ, Ñ, Ñ, add -Ð¸ instead: Ð½Ð¾Ð¶ (nozh) becomes Ð½Ð¾Ð¶Ð¸ (nozhi, knives). For masculine nouns ending in -Ñ, change to -Ð¸: ÑÐ»Ð¾Ð²Ð°ÑÑ (slovar') becomes ÑÐ»Ð¾Ð²Ð°ÑÐ¸ (slovari, dictionaries). For feminine nouns ending in -Ñ, change to -Ð¸: Ð½ÐµÐ´ÐµÐ»Ñ (nedelya) becomes Ð½ÐµÐ´ÐµÐ»Ð¸ (nedeli, weeks). For feminine nouns ending in -Ñ, change to -Ð¸: Ð´Ð²ÐµÑÑ (dver') becomes Ð´Ð²ÐµÑÐ¸ (dveri, doors). For neuter nouns ending in -Ð¾, change to -Ð°: Ð¾ÐºÐ½Ð¾ (okno) becomes Ð¾ÐºÐ½Ð° (okna, windows). In Russian, 'this' and 'that' are expressed by the words ÑÑÐ¾Ñ (etot) for masculine, ÑÑÐ° (eta) for feminine, ÑÑÐ¾ (eto) for neuter, and ÑÑÐ¸ (eti) for plural. Describing Your Room and Belongings

Module 5 · Present Tense Verbs and Simple Sentences

First Conjugation Verbs

In Russian, most verbs in the present tense follow two main conjugation patterns. First conjugation verbs are the most common. They typically end in -Ð°ÑÑ, -ÑÑÑ, or -ÐµÑÑ in their infinitive form. For example, ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°ÑÑ (to read), Ð³ÑÐ»ÑÑÑ (to walk), and Ð±Ð¾Ð»ÐµÑÑ (to be ill). To conjugate, you drop the infinitive ending and add the present tense endings: -Ñ/-Ñ, -ÐµÑÑ, -ÐµÑ, -ÐµÐ¼, -ÐµÑÐµ, -ÑÑ/-ÑÑ. Let's practice with ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°ÑÑ: Ñ ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°Ñ (I read), ÑÑ ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°ÐµÑÑ (you read), Ð¾Ð½/Ð¾Ð½Ð° ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°ÐµÑ (he/she reads), Ð¼Ñ ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°ÐµÐ¼ (we read), Ð²Ñ ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°ÐµÑÐµ (you all read), Ð¾Ð½Ð¸ ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°ÑÑ (they read). Notice the endings. For verbs ending in -Ð¾Ð²Ð°ÑÑ, like ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÐ°ÑÑ (to work), the stem changes: Ñ ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÐ°Ñ, ÑÑ ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÐ°ÐµÑÑ, etc. Also, verbs ending in -Ð°Ð²Ð°ÑÑ, like Ð´Ð°Ð²Ð°ÑÑ (to give), drop the -Ð²Ð°-: Ñ Ð´Ð°Ñ, ÑÑ Ð´Ð°ÑÑÑ, etc. In African business contexts, you might say: Ð¯ ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÐ°Ñ Ð² ÐÐ°Ð¹ÑÐ¾Ð±Ð¸ (I work in Nairobi). ÐÑ Ð¿ÑÐ¾Ð´Ð°ÑÐ¼ ÑÐ¾Ð²Ð°ÑÑ (We sell goods). ÐÐ½Ð° ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°ÐµÑ ÐºÐ¾Ð½ÑÑÐ°ÐºÑ (She reads the contract). Practice conjugating these verbs daily. Write out the forms for Ð³Ð¾Ð²Ð¾ÑÐ¸ÑÑ (to speak), Ð·Ð½Ð°ÑÑ (to know), and Ð´ÐµÐ»Ð°ÑÑ (to do). Use them in sentences about your work: Ð¯ Ð·Ð½Ð°Ñ ÑÑÐ½Ð¾Ðº (I know the market). Ð¢Ñ Ð´ÐµÐ»Ð°ÐµÑÑ ÑÐ¾ÑÐ¾ÑÑÑ ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÑ (You do good work). Remember, the present tense in Russian can also express future actions in some contexts, but for now, focus on present meaning. Negation and Questions

Negation in Russian is simple: just add Ð½Ðµ before the verb. For example, Ð¯ ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÐ°Ñ (I work) becomes Ð¯ Ð½Ðµ ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÐ°Ñ (I don't work). Verb Aspects: Introduction

Russian verbs come in pairs: imperfective and perfective. The imperfective aspect focuses on process, repetition, or ongoing action. The perfective aspect focuses on completion or a single result. In the present tense, only imperfective verbs are used. Perfective verbs have no present tense form; they have future meaning when conjugated like present. For example, ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°ÑÑ (imperfective, to read) vs Ð¿ÑÐ¾ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°ÑÑ (perfective, to read completely). Present: Ð¯ ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°Ñ ÐºÐ½Ð¸Ð³Ñ (I am reading a book). Future perfective: Ð¯ Ð¿ÑÐ¾ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°Ñ ÐºÐ½Ð¸Ð³Ñ (I will read the book). So in present tense, you always use imperfective. Common pairs: Ð´ÐµÐ»Ð°ÑÑ/ÑÐ´ÐµÐ»Ð°ÑÑ (to do), Ð¿Ð¸ÑÐ°ÑÑ/Ð½Ð°Ð¿Ð¸ÑÐ°ÑÑ (to write), Ð³Ð¾Ð²Ð¾ÑÐ¸ÑÑ/ÑÐºÐ°Ð·Ð°ÑÑ (to say), Ð¿Ð¾ÐºÑÐ¿Ð°ÑÑ/ÐºÑÐ¿Ð¸ÑÑ (to buy). In African business contexts, you might say: Ð¯ Ð¿Ð¾ÐºÑÐ¿Ð°Ñ ÑÐ¾Ð²Ð°ÑÑ ÐºÐ°Ð¶Ð´ÑÐ¹ Ð´ÐµÐ½Ñ (I buy goods every day) - imperfective, repeated action. ÐÐ°Ð²ÑÑÐ° Ñ ÐºÑÐ¿Ð»Ñ ÑÐ¾Ð²Ð°ÑÑ (Tomorrow I will buy goods) - perfective, future. Notice the different verb. To form the perfective future, conjugate like present: Ñ ÐºÑÐ¿Ð»Ñ, ÑÑ ÐºÑÐ¿Ð¸ÑÑ, etc. But for now, focus on present tense imperfective. Practice identifying pairs: ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°ÑÑ/Ð¿ÑÐ¾ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°ÑÑ, ÑÐ¼Ð¾ÑÑÐµÑÑ/Ð¿Ð¾ÑÐ¼Ð¾ÑÑÐµÑÑ, Ð³Ð¾ÑÐ¾Ð²Ð¸ÑÑ/Ð¿ÑÐ¸Ð³Ð¾ÑÐ¾Ð²Ð¸ÑÑ. Use imperfective in present: Ð¯ Ð³Ð¾ÑÐ¾Ð²Ð»Ñ Ð¾Ð±ÐµÐ´ (I am preparing lunch). Ð¯ ÑÐ¼Ð¾ÑÑÑ Ð½Ð¾Ð²Ð¾ÑÑÐ¸ (I am watching news). Then imagine future perfective: Ð¯ Ð¿ÑÐ¸Ð³Ð¾ÑÐ¾Ð²Ð»Ñ Ð¾Ð±ÐµÐ´ (I will prepare lunch). Ð¯ Ð¿Ð¾ÑÐ¼Ð¾ÑÑÑ Ð½Ð¾Ð²Ð¾ÑÑÐ¸ (I will watch news). This distinction is crucial. Start with 10 common verb pairs. Write sentences in present imperfective. For example: Ð¯ ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÐ°Ñ (I work) - imperfective. Ð¯ ÑÐ´ÐµÐ»Ð°Ñ (I will do) - perfective future. Don't mix them up. Practice with flashcards. Listen to how native speakers use aspects in context. Soon, you'll grasp the logic. Spend 15 minutes daily on aspect pairs..Trim()

Building Simple Sentences

Russian sentence structure is flexible, but the basic pattern is Subject-Verb-Object, similar to English. However, word order can change for emphasis. In present tense, the verb 'to be' is omitted. So 'I am a student' is Ð¯ ÑÑÑÐ´ÐµÐ½Ñ (I student). 'She is a doctor' is ÐÐ½Ð° Ð²ÑÐ°Ñ. For possession, use Ð£ Ð¼ÐµÐ½Ñ ÐµÑÑÑ... (I have...). Literally 'By me there is...'. For example: Ð£ Ð¼ÐµÐ½Ñ ÐµÑÑÑ Ð±Ð¸Ð·Ð½ÐµÑ (I have a business). Ð£ ÑÐµÐ±Ñ ÐµÑÑÑ Ð²ÑÐµÐ¼Ñ? (Do you have time?) - answer: ÐÐ°, Ñ Ð¼ÐµÐ½Ñ ÐµÑÑÑ (Yes, I have) or ÐÐµÑ, Ñ Ð¼ÐµÐ½Ñ Ð½ÐµÑ (No, I don't have). To say 'I don't have', use Ð£ Ð¼ÐµÐ½Ñ Ð½ÐµÑ... (By me there is not...). For example: Ð£ Ð¼ÐµÐ½Ñ Ð½ÐµÑ Ð´ÐµÐ½ÐµÐ³ (I have no money). In African business contexts: Ð£ Ð½Ð°Ñ ÐµÑÑÑ Ð¾ÑÐ¸Ñ Ð² ÐÐºÐºÑÐµ (We have an office in Accra). Ð£ Ð½Ð¸Ñ ÐµÑÑÑ ÐºÐ»Ð¸ÐµÐ½ÑÑ (They have clients). Ð£ Ð²Ð°Ñ ÐµÑÑÑ Ð²Ð¾Ð¿ÑÐ¾ÑÑ? (Do you have questions?) - answer: ÐÐ°, Ñ Ð½Ð°Ñ ÐµÑÑÑ (Yes, we have). Also, adjectives agree with nouns in gender, number, and case. For now, learn nominative case: Ð½Ð¾Ð²ÑÐ¹ ÑÐ¾Ð²Ð°Ñ (new goods), Ð½Ð¾Ð²Ð°Ñ Ð¼Ð°ÑÐ¸Ð½Ð° (new car), Ð½Ð¾Ð²Ð¾Ðµ Ð·Ð´Ð°Ð½Ð¸Ðµ (new building), Ð½Ð¾Ð²ÑÐµ ÑÐ¾Ð²Ð°ÑÑ (new goods plural). Practice building sentences: Ð¯ ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÐ°Ñ Ð² Ð¾ÑÐ¸ÑÐµ (I work in an office). ÐÑ Ð¿ÑÐ¾Ð´Ð°ÑÐ¼ ÑÐ¾Ð²Ð°ÑÑ (We sell goods). ÐÐ½Ð° Ð³Ð¾Ð²Ð¾ÑÐ¸Ñ Ð¿Ð¾-ÑÑÑÑÐºÐ¸ (She speaks Russian). Use conjunctions: Ð¸ (and), Ð½Ð¾ (but), Ð¸Ð»Ð¸ (or). For example: Ð¯ ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÐ°Ñ Ð¸ ÑÑÑÑÑ (I work and study). ÐÐ½ Ð³Ð¾Ð²Ð¾ÑÐ¸Ñ Ð¿Ð¾-Ð°Ð½Ð³Ð»Ð¸Ð¹ÑÐºÐ¸, Ð½Ð¾ Ð½Ðµ Ð¿Ð¾-ÑÑÑÑÐºÐ¸ (He speaks English but not Russian). Write 10 sentences about your daily life. Use subject, verb, object. Then expand with time expressions: ÑÐµÐ³Ð¾Ð´Ð½Ñ (today), ÐºÐ°Ð¶Ð´ÑÐ¹ Ð´ÐµÐ½Ñ (every day), ÑÑÑÐ¾Ð¼ (in the morning). For example: Ð¯ Ð¿ÑÑ ÑÐ°Ð¹ ÑÑÑÐ¾Ð¼ (I drink tea in the morning). Russian Cuisine and Traditional Dishes

Russian cuisine is hearty and uses simple ingredients. Start with soups: Ð±Ð¾ÑÑ (borscht) is beetroot soup, often with cabbage, potatoes, and meat. Served with sour cream 'ÑÐ¼ÐµÑÐ°Ð½Ð°'. Ð©Ð¸ (shchi) is cabbage soup. Ð£ÑÐ° (ukha) is fish soup. For main dishes, Ð¿ÐµÐ»ÑÐ¼ÐµÐ½Ð¸ (pelmeni) are dumplings filled with meat, eaten with butter or sour cream. ÐÐ°ÑÐµÐ½Ð¸ÐºÐ¸ (vareniki) are similar but often filled with potato, cheese, or cherries. ÐÐ»Ð¸Ð½Ñ (blini) are thin pancakes, served with caviar, jam, or sour cream. ÐÐ¸ÑÐ¾Ð¶ÐºÐ¸ (pirozhki) are stuffed buns. ÐÐ°ÑÐ° (kasha) is porridge, made from buckwheat, rice, or oats. For protein, ÑÐ°ÑÐ»ÑÐº (shashlik) is marinated meat grilled on skewers. ÐÐ»Ð¾Ð² (plov) is rice pilaf with meat and carrots. Ð¡ÐµÐ»ÑÐ´ÐºÐ° Ð¿Ð¾Ð´ ÑÑÐ±Ð¾Ð¹ (herring under a fur coat) is a layered salad with herring, potatoes, carrots, beets, and mayonnaise. For drinks, ÐºÐ¾Ð¼Ð¿Ð¾Ñ (kompot) is fruit juice, ÐºÐ²Ð°Ñ (kvas) is fermented bread drink, and ÑÐ°Ð¹ (tea) is common. Vodka 'Ð²Ð¾Ð´ÐºÐ°' is popular but strong. In African context, you can adapt: use local ingredients. For borscht, use beetroot and cabbage available in your market. For pelmeni, use ground beef or chicken. For shashlik, use goat or beef. Learn adjectives: Ð²ÐºÑÑÐ½ÑÐ¹ (tasty), ÑÐ»Ð°Ð´ÐºÐ¸Ð¹ (sweet), ÑÐ¾Ð»ÑÐ½ÑÐ¹ (salty), Ð¾ÑÑÑÑÐ¹ (spicy), ÐºÐ¸ÑÐ»ÑÐ¹ (sour). Say 'ÐÐ½Ðµ Ð½ÑÐ°Ð²Ð¸ÑÑÑ Ð±Ð¾ÑÑ' (I like borscht). Accusative Case for Direct Objects

The accusative case is used for the direct object of a sentenceâthe thing that receives the action. In Russian, if you say 'I see the book', 'book' is the direct object. In Russian, 'Ð¯ Ð²Ð¸Ð¶Ñ ÐºÐ½Ð¸Ð³Ñ' (kniga becomes knigu). For masculine nouns that are inanimate (not alive), the accusative is the same as the nominative (subject) form. For example, 'Ð¯ Ð²Ð¸Ð¶Ñ ÑÑÐ¾Ð»' (I see the table) â ÑÑÐ¾Ð» doesn't change. For animate masculine nouns (people and animals), the accusative takes the genitive ending -Ð°/-Ñ. For example, 'Ð¯ Ð²Ð¸Ð¶Ñ Ð±ÑÐ°ÑÐ°' (I see brother) â Ð±ÑÐ°Ñ becomes Ð±ÑÐ°ÑÐ°. For feminine nouns ending in -Ð°/-Ñ, the accusative changes -Ð° to -Ñ, -Ñ to -Ñ. For example, 'Ð¯ Ð²Ð¸Ð¶Ñ ÐºÐ½Ð¸Ð³Ñ' (I see the book), 'Ð¯ Ð²Ð¸Ð¶Ñ Ð½ÐµÐ´ÐµÐ»Ñ' (I see the week). For neuter nouns, the accusative is the same as the nominative. For example, 'Ð¯ Ð²Ð¸Ð¶Ñ Ð¾ÐºÐ½Ð¾' (I see the window). For plural nouns, the accusative depends on animacy. Inanimate plurals are the same as nominative. Animate plurals take genitive plural endings. For example, 'Ð¯ Ð²Ð¸Ð¶Ñ ÑÑÐ¾Ð»Ñ' (I see tables) â same as nominative. 'Ð¯ Ð²Ð¸Ð¶Ñ Ð±ÑÐ°ÑÑÐµÐ²' (I see brothers) â genitive plural. Now apply to food and shopping. When you order food, you use accusative: 'Ð¯ ÑÐ¾ÑÑ ÑÑÐ±Ñ' (I want fish) â ÑÑÐ±Ð° becomes ÑÑÐ±Ñ. 'Ð¯ ÐºÑÐ¿Ð»Ñ ÐºÐ°ÑÑÐ¾ÑÐºÑ' (I will buy potatoes) â ÐºÐ°ÑÑÐ¾ÑÐºÐ° becomes ÐºÐ°ÑÑÐ¾ÑÐºÑ. 'ÐÐ°Ð¹ÑÐµ ÑÐ»ÐµÐ±' (Give me bread) â ÑÐ»ÐµÐ± is masculine inanimate, so no change. 'Ð¯ Ð»ÑÐ±Ð»Ñ ÐºÑÑÐ¸ÑÑ' (I love chicken) â ÐºÑÑÐ¸ÑÐ° becomes ÐºÑÑÐ¸ÑÑ. Practice with these sentences: 'Ð¯ ÐµÐ¼ ÑÐ±Ð»Ð¾ÐºÐ¾' (I eat an apple) â neuter, no change. 'Ð¯ Ð¿ÑÑ Ð²Ð¾Ð´Ñ' (I drink water) â Ð²Ð¾Ð´Ð° becomes Ð²Ð¾Ð´Ñ. 'Ð¯ Ð²Ð¸Ð¶Ñ ÐºÐ¾ÑÐºÑ' (I see a cat) â ÐºÐ¾ÑÐºÐ° becomes ÐºÐ¾ÑÐºÑ. 'Ð¯ Ð·Ð½Ð°Ñ ÐÐ²Ð°Ð½Ð°' (I know Ivan) â ÐÐ²Ð°Ð½ becomes ÐÐ²Ð°Ð½Ð°. Make a list of 20 food items and practice using them as direct objects. For example, 'Ð¯ Ð¿Ð¾ÐºÑÐ¿Ð°Ñ Ð¼Ð¾Ð»Ð¾ÐºÐ¾' (I buy milk) â neuter, no change. 'Ð¯ Ð³Ð¾ÑÐ¾Ð²Ð»Ñ ÑÑÐ±Ñ' (I cook fish) â feminine, change to ÑÑÐ±Ñ. 'Ð¯ ÑÐµÐ¶Ñ Ð»ÑÐº' (I cut onion) â masculine inanimate, no change. Do this daily for 10 minutes. Write sentences. Say them aloud. Record yourself. Compare with native speakers on Forvo. This case is essential for speaking correctly. Without it, you'll sound foreign. With practice, it becomes automatic..Trim()

Role-play: At the Market

This role-play simulates a visit to a Russian market 'ÑÑÐ½Ð¾Ðº'. You will play both buyer and seller. First, set up a market stall with items: fruits, vegetables, bread, meat, and drinks. Seller: 'ÐÐ°ÑÐ° ÑÐ´Ð°ÑÐ° 150 ÑÑÐ±Ð»ÐµÐ¹. Ð¡Ð¿Ð°ÑÐ¸Ð±Ð¾ Ð·Ð° Ð¿Ð¾ÐºÑÐ¿ÐºÑ!' (Your change is 150 rubles. Thank you for your purchase!) Buyer: 'Ð¡Ð¿Ð°ÑÐ¸Ð±Ð¾! ÐÐ¾ ÑÐ²Ð¸Ð´Ð°Ð½Ð¸Ñ!' If you don't like the price, say 'ÐÐ¾ÑÐ¾Ð³Ð¾' (Expensive) and walk away. If you want to buy more, 'ÐÐ°Ð¹ÑÐµ ÑÑÐ¸ ÐºÐ¸Ð»Ð¾Ð³ÑÐ°Ð¼Ð¼Ð°' (Give me three kilograms). For payment, use cash or card. Module 8 · Getting Around: Directions and Transportation

City Places and Landmarks

When you land in a Russian city like Moscow or St. Petersburg, or even smaller ones like Kazan or Sochi, the first thing you need is to know the names of key places. Start with these 10 words: ÑÐ»Ð¸ÑÐ° (ulitsa) = street, Ð¿ÑÐ¾ÑÐ¿ÐµÐºÑ (prospekt) = avenue, Ð¿Ð»Ð¾ÑÐ°Ð´Ñ (ploshchad) = square, Ð¼ÐµÑÑÐ¾ (metro) = subway, Ð²Ð¾ÐºÐ·Ð°Ð» (vokzal) = train station, Ð°ÑÑÐ¾Ð¿Ð¾ÑÑ (aeroport) = airport, Ð°Ð²ÑÐ¾Ð±ÑÑÐ½Ð°Ñ Ð¾ÑÑÐ°Ð½Ð¾Ð²ÐºÐ° (avtobusnaya ostanovka) = bus stop, ÑÑÐ½Ð¾Ðº (rynok) = market, Ð°Ð¿ÑÐµÐºÐ° (apteka) = pharmacy, Ð±Ð°Ð½Ðº (bank) = bank. .Trim()

Use landmarks to orient yourself. In many Russian cities, the main street is called 'ÐÐµÐ½Ð¸Ð½ÑÐºÐ¸Ð¹ Ð¿ÑÐ¾ÑÐ¿ÐµÐºÑ' (Leninsky prospekt) or 'ÑÐ»Ð¸ÑÐ° ÐÐµÐ½Ð¸Ð½Ð°' (ulitsa Lenina). The central square is often 'Ð¿Ð»Ð¾ÑÐ°Ð´Ñ ÐÐµÐ½Ð¸Ð½Ð°' (ploshchad Lenina). If you're lost, look for the metro sign: a big 'Ð'. That's your anchor. In Africa, we often use roundabouts or big malls as landmarks; in Russia, metro stations and statues of Lenin serve the same purpose. For example, 'Ð¯ Ð½Ð°ÑÐ¾Ð¶ÑÑÑ Ñ Ð¿Ð°Ð¼ÑÑÐ½Ð¸ÐºÐ° ÐÑÑÐºÐ¸Ð½Ñ' (Ya nakhodus u pamyatnika Pushkinu) means 'I am at the Pushkin monument.'.Trim()

.Trim()

When asking for a place, use the phrase 'ÐÐ´Ðµ Ð½Ð°ÑÐ¾Ð´Ð¸ÑÑÑ ...?' (Gde nakhoditsya ...?) meaning 'Where is ...?' For example, 'ÐÐ´Ðµ Ð½Ð°ÑÐ¾Ð´Ð¸ÑÑÑ Ð°Ð¿ÑÐµÐºÐ°?' Asking for and Giving Directions

Asking for directions in Russian is straightforward if you know a few key phrases. Start with 'ÐÐ·Ð²Ð¸Ð½Ð¸ÑÐµ, Ð²Ñ Ð½Ðµ Ð¿Ð¾Ð´ÑÐºÐ°Ð¶ÐµÑÐµ, Ð³Ð´Ðµ ...?' In Africa, we often greet before asking; in Russia, a simple 'ÐÐ·Ð²Ð¸Ð½Ð¸ÑÐµ' is enough, but a smile helps..Trim()

.Trim()

When giving directions, use these verbs: Ð¸Ð´ÑÐ¸ (idti) = to go on foot, ÐµÑÐ°ÑÑ (yekhat') = to go by vehicle. For example, 'ÐÐ´Ð¸ÑÐµ Ð¿ÑÑÐ¼Ð¾' (Go straight), 'ÐÐ¾Ð²ÐµÑÐ½Ð¸ÑÐµ Ð½Ð°Ð»ÐµÐ²Ð¾' (Turn left), 'ÐÐ¾Ð²ÐµÑÐ½Ð¸ÑÐµ Ð½Ð°Ð¿ÑÐ°Ð²Ð¾' (Turn right). To say 'It's on the left/right', use 'Ð¡Ð»ÐµÐ²Ð°' (Sleva) / 'Ð¡Ð¿ÑÐ°Ð²Ð°' (Sprava). To say 'It's near/far', use 'ÐÐ»Ð¸Ð·ÐºÐ¾' (Blizko) / 'ÐÐ°Ð»ÐµÐºÐ¾' (Daleko). For distances, use 'Ð¼ÐµÑÑÐ¾Ð²' (metrov) for meters: 'ÐÑÐ¾Ð¹Ð´Ð¸ÑÐµ 200 Ð¼ÐµÑÑÐ¾Ð²' (Walk 200 meters). If you need to say 'It's across the street', say 'Ð§ÐµÑÐµÐ· Ð´Ð¾ÑÐ¾Ð³Ñ' (Cherez dorogu)..Trim()

Listening to directions can be tricky because Russians speak fast. Prepositional Case for Location

The prepositional case is used to say where something is located. In Russian, you change the ending of nouns and use prepositions 'Ð²' (v) or 'Ð½Ð°' (na) meaning 'in' or 'at'. For most masculine nouns, add '-Ðµ' (e): Ð² Ð±Ð°Ð½ÐºÐµ (v banke) = in the bank, Ð½Ð° ÑÑÐ½ÐºÐµ (na rynke) = at the market. For feminine nouns ending in '-Ð°', change to '-Ðµ': Ð² Ð°Ð¿ÑÐµÐºÐµ (v apteke) = in the pharmacy, Ð½Ð° Ð¿Ð»Ð¾ÑÐ°Ð´Ð¸ (na ploshchadi) = on the square (note: Ð¿Ð»Ð¾ÑÐ°Ð´Ñ ends in soft sign, so add '-Ð¸'). For neuter nouns ending in '-Ð¾', change to '-Ðµ': Ð² Ð¼ÐµÑÑÐ¾ (v metro) = in the metro (no change because it's foreign), Ð² Ð¾ÐºÐ½Ðµ (v okne) = in the window. For plural nouns, add '-Ð°Ñ' or '-ÑÑ': Ð² Ð±Ð°Ð½ÐºÐ°Ñ (v bankakh) = in the banks, Ð½Ð° ÑÐ»Ð¸ÑÐ°Ñ (na ulitsakh) = on the streets..Trim()

.Trim()

Key prepositions: 'Ð²' is used for enclosed spaces (Ð² Ð¼Ð°Ð³Ð°Ð·Ð¸Ð½Ðµ = in the store, Ð² Ð¿Ð°ÑÐºÐµ = in the park). 'ÐÐ°' is used for open spaces, surfaces, and some fixed expressions (Ð½Ð° ÑÐ»Ð¸ÑÐµ = on the street, Ð½Ð° Ð¿Ð¾ÑÑÐµ = at the post office, Ð½Ð° Ð²Ð¾ÐºÐ·Ð°Ð»Ðµ = at the train station). Remember: Ð² Ð Ð¾ÑÑÐ¸Ð¸ (v Rossii) = in Russia, Ð½Ð° ÐÑÐ±Ðµ (na Kube) = in Cuba. For African countries, use 'Ð²': Ð² ÐÐµÐ½Ð¸Ð¸ (v Kenii) = in Kenya, Ð² ÐÐ¸Ð³ÐµÑÐ¸Ð¸ (v Nigerii) = in Nigeria. So 'Ð¯ Ð¶Ð¸Ð²Ñ Ð² ÐÐµÐ½Ð¸Ð¸' (I live in Kenya)..Trim()

.Trim()

To ask 'Where is ...?' use 'ÐÐ´Ðµ ...?' with the prepositional case: ÐÐ´Ðµ Ð±Ð°Ð½Ðº? (Where is the bank?) but if you want to say 'Where is the bank located?' you can say 'ÐÐ´Ðµ Ð½Ð°ÑÐ¾Ð´Ð¸ÑÑÑ Ð±Ð°Ð½Ðº?' Practice with common places: Ð² Ð°ÑÑÐ¾Ð¿Ð¾ÑÑÑ (at the airport), Ð½Ð° Ð²Ð¾ÐºÐ·Ð°Ð»Ðµ (at the train station), Ð² Ð³Ð¾ÑÑÐ¸Ð½Ð¸ÑÐµ (in the hotel), Ð½Ð° Ð¾ÑÑÐ°Ð½Ð¾Ð²ÐºÐµ (at the bus stop)..Trim()

Drill yourself: take a list of 20 places and say where you are. For example, 'Ð¯ Ð² Ð¼ÐµÑÑÐ¾' (I am in the metro), 'Ð¯ Ð½Ð° ÑÑÐ½ÐºÐµ' (I am at the market), 'Ð¯ Ð² Ð±Ð°Ð½ÐºÐµ' (I am in the bank). Then change to 'ÐÐ½ Ð² Ð¼ÐµÑÑÐ¾' (He is in the metro), 'ÐÐ½Ð° Ð½Ð° ÑÑÐ½ÐºÐµ' (She is at the market). Use flashcards. In Africa, we often say 'I'm at the bank' without changing the word; in Russian, the ending changes. So memorize the pattern: masculine -Ðµ, feminine -Ðµ (or -Ð¸ for soft sign), neuter -Ðµ (often unchanged). With practice, it becomes automatic..Trim()

Public Transportation Vocabulary

Russia has excellent public transportation, especially in big cities. The metro (Ð¼ÐµÑÑÐ¾) is the fastest and cheapest. In Moscow, a single ride costs around 60 rubles (about $0.70), and you can buy a Troika card for 50 rubles and load it with any amount. Buses (Ð°Ð²ÑÐ¾Ð±ÑÑÑ), trolleybuses (ÑÑÐ¾Ð»Ð»ÐµÐ¹Ð±ÑÑÑ), and trams (ÑÑÐ°Ð¼Ð²Ð°Ð¸) cost about 60 rubles per ride. Marshrutkas (Ð¼Ð°ÑÑÑÑÑÐºÐ¸) are shared minivans that follow fixed routes; you pay the driver directly, usually 50-70 rubles. In Africa, we have matatus and danfos; marshrutkas are similar but more regulated. Always have small change..Trim()

.Trim()

Key vocabulary: Ð¾ÑÑÐ°Ð½Ð¾Ð²ÐºÐ° (ostanovka) = stop, Ð±Ð¸Ð»ÐµÑ (bilet) = ticket, Ð¿ÑÐ¾ÐµÐ·Ð´Ð½Ð¾Ð¹ (proyezdnoy) = travel pass, ÐºÐ¾Ð½ÑÑÐ¾Ð»ÑÑ (kontrolor) = ticket inspector, Ð²Ð°Ð³Ð¾Ð½ (vagon) = train car, ÑÑÐ°Ð½ÑÐ¸Ñ (stantsiya) = station, Ð¿ÐµÑÐµÑÐ°Ð´ÐºÐ° (peresadka) = transfer, Ð²ÑÑÐ¾Ð´ (vykhod) = exit, Ð²ÑÐ¾Ð´ (vkhod) = entrance. (How many stops?). 'ÐÐ½Ðµ Ð½ÑÐ¶Ð½Ð¾ Ð²ÑÐ¹ÑÐ¸ Ð½Ð° ÑÐ»ÐµÐ´ÑÑÑÐµÐ¹ Ð¾ÑÑÐ°Ð½Ð¾Ð²ÐºÐµ' (I need to get off at the next stop)..Trim()

.Trim()

When you board a bus or tram, you can pay the driver or use a validator (ÐºÐ¾Ð¼Ð¿Ð¾ÑÑÐµÑ) to stamp your ticket. In Moscow, you can also pay by bank card or phone. In smaller cities, you often pay the driver in cash. Keep your ticket until you exit; inspectors (ÐºÐ¾Ð½ÑÑÐ¾Ð»ÑÑÑ) can fine you 1000 rubles for not having a valid ticket. On the metro, you swipe your Troika card at the turnstile. .Trim()

Practice by planning a route. Use Yandex Maps or the app 'ÐÐµÑÑÐ¾ ÐÐ¾ÑÐºÐ²Ñ' (Moscow Metro) to find how to get from your hotel to a landmark. For example, from 'ÐÐµÐ»Ð¾ÑÑÑÑÐºÐ°Ñ' to 'ÐÐ°ÑÐº ÐÑÐ»ÑÑÑÑÑ' you take the brown line (ÐºÐ¾Ð»ÑÑÐµÐ²Ð°Ñ) and it's 4 stops. Write down the names. Buying Tickets and Traveling

Buying tickets for trains, planes, and buses in Russian is a practical skill. Start with the word 'Ð±Ð¸Ð»ÐµÑ' (bilet) = ticket. For train tickets, go to the 'ÐºÐ°ÑÑÐ°' (kassa) = ticket office at the 'Ð²Ð¾ÐºÐ·Ð°Ð»' (vokzal) = train station. Say: 'ÐÐ½Ðµ Ð½ÑÐ¶ÐµÐ½ Ð±Ð¸Ð»ÐµÑ Ð´Ð¾ ...' (I need a ticket to ...). Prices vary: Moscow to St. Petersburg by train costs from 1500 rubles (about $18) for platzkart to 5000 rubles for a compartment. By plane, from 2500 rubles. Always ask 'Ð¡ÐºÐ¾Ð»ÑÐºÐ¾ ÑÑÐ¾Ð¸Ñ?' (How much does it cost?)..Trim()

.Trim()

For buses, you can buy tickets at the bus station (Ð°Ð²ÑÐ¾Ð²Ð¾ÐºÐ·Ð°Ð») or online. In Russia, use 'Ð¯Ð½Ð´ÐµÐºÑ.ÐÑÑÐµÑÐµÑÑÐ²Ð¸Ñ' or 'BlaBlaCar' for intercity travel. For local buses, pay the driver. For metro, buy a Troika card or a single-use token (Ð¶ÐµÑÐ¾Ð½). In Africa, we often negotiate prices; in Russia, prices are fixed, so don't bargain. Just pay the stated amount. If you need a return ticket, say 'ÑÑÐ´Ð° Ð¸ Ð¾Ð±ÑÐ°ÑÐ½Ð¾' (there and back). For example, 'ÐÐ½Ðµ Ð½ÑÐ¶ÐµÐ½ Ð±Ð¸Ð»ÐµÑ ÑÑÐ´Ð° Ð¸ Ð¾Ð±ÑÐ°ÑÐ½Ð¾ Ð² ÐÐ°Ð·Ð°Ð½Ñ' (I need a round-trip ticket to Kazan)..Trim()

.Trim()

When traveling, keep your passport and visa (if required) handy. For domestic flights, you need a passport. For international, check visa requirements. In Russia, you must register your visa within 7 days of arrival. Hotels do this automatically, but if you stay in a private apartment, you must do it yourself at the post office. Always carry your passport; police can ask for it. For train travel, you need your passport to board. The conductor (Ð¿ÑÐ¾Ð²Ð¾Ð´Ð½Ð¸Ðº) will check it. On the train, you get a bed, and for long trips, bring food and water. Tea (ÑÐ°Ð¹) is available from the conductor for a small fee..Trim()

.Trim()

Practice by booking a fictional trip. Go to a Russian train website (rzd.ru) and find a route from Moscow to Sochi. Note the prices and times. Learn to say 'Ð¯ ÑÐ¾ÑÑ ÐºÑÐ¿Ð¸ÑÑ Ð±Ð¸Ð»ÐµÑ' (I want to buy a ticket), 'ÐÐ¾Ð³Ð´Ð° ÑÐ»ÐµÐ´ÑÑÑÐ¸Ð¹ Ð¿Ð¾ÐµÐ·Ð´?' (When is the next train?), 'Ð¡ ÐºÐ°ÐºÐ¾Ð³Ð¾ Ð²Ð¾ÐºÐ·Ð°Ð»Ð°?' (From which station?). In Africa, we often use buses for long distances; in Russia, trains are more comfortable for overnight trips. For example, the Moscow-Vladivostok train takes 6 days. So plan ahead. Now you can travel like a local..Trim()

Practical: Navigating a Russian City

Now put everything together. Imagine you are in Moscow, near Red Square. You need to get to the Tretyakov Gallery. .Trim()

Module 9 · Past Tense and Narrating Events

Forming the Past Tense

In Russian, the past tense is formed by taking the infinitive (the dictionary form of the verb) and adding a suffix that agrees with the subject in gender and number. For most verbs, you remove the -ÑÑ ending and add -Ð» for masculine singular, -Ð»Ð° for feminine singular, -Ð»Ð¾ for neuter singular, and -Ð»Ð¸ for plural. For example, ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°ÑÑ (to read) becomes ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°Ð» (he read), ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°Ð»Ð° (she read), ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°Ð»Ð¾ (it read), ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°Ð»Ð¸ (they read). This pattern works for the majority of verbs, including many common ones like ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÐ°ÑÑ (to work) â ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÐ°Ð», Ð³Ð¾Ð²Ð¾ÑÐ¸ÑÑ (to speak) â Ð³Ð¾Ð²Ð¾ÑÐ¸Ð», and ÑÐ¼Ð¾ÑÑÐµÑÑ (to watch) â ÑÐ¼Ð¾ÑÑÐµÐ». .Trim()

.Trim()

To use the past tense in real conversations, you need to match the verb to the subject. If you are a man, you say Ð¯ ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÐ°Ð» (I worked). If you are a woman, you say Ð¯ ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÐ°Ð»Ð°. For plural subjects like Ð¼Ñ (we), Ð²Ñ (you all), or Ð¾Ð½Ð¸ (they), you use -Ð»Ð¸: ÐÑ ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÐ°Ð»Ð¸ (We worked). This is different from English, where the verb doesn't change with gender. So pay attention: in Russian, the speaker's gender is shown in the past tense. .Trim()

.Trim()

Let's practice with a typical African business scenario. Imagine you are a market vendor in Lagos or Nairobi. You might say: ÐÑÐµÑÐ° Ñ Ð¿ÑÐ¾Ð´Ð°Ð²Ð°Ð» ÑÐ¾Ð²Ð°ÑÑ Ð½Ð° ÑÑÐ½ÐºÐµ (Yesterday I sold goods at the market) if you are male, or ÐÑÐµÑÐ° Ñ Ð¿ÑÐ¾Ð´Ð°Ð²Ð°Ð»Ð° ÑÐ¾Ð²Ð°ÑÑ Ð½Ð° ÑÑÐ½ÐºÐµ if you are female. If you and your partner worked together, you say: ÐÑ Ð¿ÑÐ¾Ð´Ð°Ð²Ð°Ð»Ð¸ ÑÐ¾Ð²Ð°ÑÑ Ð²ÐµÑÑ Ð´ÐµÐ½Ñ (We sold goods all day). Notice how the verb changes. .Trim()

.Trim()

Common mistakes: Forgetting to change the ending for gender. Many learners say Ð¯ ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÐ°Ð» even when they are female. Also, don't confuse the past tense with the present tense. The present tense uses different endings (e.g., Ñ ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÐ°Ñ). The past tense is simpler because it doesn't change for person (I, you, he, she) except for gender and number. So, learn the four endings: -Ð», -Ð»Ð°, -Ð»Ð¾, -Ð»Ð¸. Practice by describing what you did yesterday: Ð¯ Ð²ÑÑÐ°Ð» ÑÐ°Ð½Ð¾ (I got up early â male), Ð¯ Ð²ÑÑÐ°Ð»Ð° ÑÐ°Ð½Ð¾ (female), ÐÑ Ð²ÑÑÐ°Ð»Ð¸ ÑÐ°Ð½Ð¾ (we). With regular practice, you'll master this quickly..Trim()

Irregular Past Tense Verbs

While most Russian verbs form the past tense regularly, a few common verbs are irregular. The most important is Ð±ÑÑÑ (to be). In the past tense, it becomes Ð±ÑÐ» (masculine), Ð±ÑÐ»Ð° (feminine), Ð±ÑÐ»Ð¾ (neuter), Ð±ÑÐ»Ð¸ (plural). For example: Ð¯ Ð±ÑÐ» Ð² ÐÐ¾ÑÐºÐ²Ðµ (I was in Moscow â male), Ð¯ Ð±ÑÐ»Ð° Ð² ÐÐ¾ÑÐºÐ²Ðµ (female), ÐÐ½Ð¸ Ð±ÑÐ»Ð¸ Ð² ÐÐ¾ÑÐºÐ²Ðµ (They were in Moscow). This verb is used to talk about existence or location in the past. .Trim()

.Trim()

Another irregular verb is Ð¸Ð´ÑÐ¸ (to go on foot). Its past tense is ÑÑÐ» (masculine), ÑÐ»Ð° (feminine), ÑÐ»Ð¾ (neuter), ÑÐ»Ð¸ (plural). Note the Ñ instead of Ð¸Ð´. For example: ÐÑÐµÑÐ° Ñ ÑÑÐ» Ð´Ð¾Ð¼Ð¾Ð¹ (Yesterday I was walking home â male). ÐÐ½Ð° ÑÐ»Ð° Ð½Ð° ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÑ (She was walking to work). ÐÑ ÑÐ»Ð¸ Ð¿Ð¾ ÑÐ»Ð¸ÑÐµ (We were walking along the street). Similarly, ÐµÑÐ°ÑÑ (to go by vehicle) becomes ÐµÑÐ°Ð», ÐµÑÐ°Ð»Ð°, ÐµÑÐ°Ð»Ð¾, ÐµÑÐ°Ð»Ð¸. .Trim()

Other common irregulars include Ð¼Ð¾ÑÑ (to be able) â Ð¼Ð¾Ð³, Ð¼Ð¾Ð³Ð»Ð°, Ð¼Ð¾Ð³Ð»Ð¾, Ð¼Ð¾Ð³Ð»Ð¸; and ÐµÑÑÑ (to eat) â ÐµÐ», ÐµÐ»Ð°, ÐµÐ»Ð¾, ÐµÐ»Ð¸. .Trim()

Talking About Yesterday and Last Week

To talk about past events, you need time expressions that set the context. .Trim()

When you use these time expressions, the verb must be in the past tense. So, you combine the time phrase with the correct past form. For instance, if you want to say 'I sold tomatoes yesterday', you say: ÐÑÐµÑÐ° Ñ Ð¿ÑÐ¾Ð´Ð°Ð²Ð°Ð» Ð¿Ð¾Ð¼Ð¸Ð´Ð¾ÑÑ (male) or ÐÑÐµÑÐ° Ñ Ð¿ÑÐ¾Ð´Ð°Ð²Ð°Ð»Ð° Ð¿Ð¾Ð¼Ð¸Ð´Ð¾ÑÑ (female). .Trim()

Practice with a typical African small business scenario. Imagine you are a tailor in Accra. You might say: ÐÑÐµÑÐ° Ñ ÑÐ¸Ð» Ð¿Ð»Ð°ÑÑÐµ (Yesterday I sewed a dress â male). ÐÐ¾Ð·Ð°Ð²ÑÐµÑÐ° Ñ ÑÐ¸Ð»Ð° ÑÑÐ±Ð°ÑÐºÑ (The day before yesterday I sewed a shirt â female). ÐÐ° Ð¿ÑÐ¾ÑÐ»Ð¾Ð¹ Ð½ÐµÐ´ÐµÐ»Ðµ Ð¼Ñ ÑÐ¸Ð»Ð¸ ÑÐºÐ¾Ð»ÑÐ½ÑÑ ÑÐ¾ÑÐ¼Ñ (Last week we sewed school uniforms). These sentences show how the verb changes with gender and number. .Trim()

.Trim()

Also, you can use these time expressions to ask questions. For example: Ð§ÑÐ¾ ÑÑ Ð´ÐµÐ»Ð°Ð» Ð²ÑÐµÑÐ°? (What did you do yesterday? â to a male). Ð§ÑÐ¾ ÑÑ Ð´ÐµÐ»Ð°Ð»Ð° Ð²ÑÐµÑÐ°? ÐÐ° Ð¿ÑÐ¾ÑÐ»Ð¾Ð¹ Ð½ÐµÐ´ÐµÐ»Ðµ Ð¼Ñ ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÐ°Ð»Ð¸ Ð² Ð¿Ð¾Ð»Ðµ (Last week we worked in the field). .Trim()

.Trim()

Remember that Ð²ÑÐµÑÐ° and Ð½Ð° Ð¿ÑÐ¾ÑÐ»Ð¾Ð¹ Ð½ÐµÐ´ÐµÐ»Ðµ are fixed phrases. Don't say Ð² Ð¿ÑÐ¾ÑÐ»Ð°Ñ Ð½ÐµÐ´ÐµÐ»Ñ â it's Ð½Ð° Ð¿ÑÐ¾ÑÐ»Ð¾Ð¹ Ð½ÐµÐ´ÐµÐ»Ðµ. Sequencing Events with Time Expressions

When narrating a story, you need to show the order of events. In Russian, you can use time expressions like ÑÐ½Ð°ÑÐ°Ð»Ð° (first), Ð¿Ð¾ÑÐ¾Ð¼ (then), Ð·Ð°ÑÐµÐ¼ (then, next), Ð¿Ð¾ÑÐ»Ðµ ÑÑÐ¾Ð³Ð¾ (after that), and Ð½Ð°ÐºÐ¾Ð½ÐµÑ (finally). These words help your listener follow your story. For example: Ð¡Ð½Ð°ÑÐ°Ð»Ð° Ñ Ð¿Ð¾Ð·Ð°Ð²ÑÑÐ°ÐºÐ°Ð», Ð¿Ð¾ÑÐ¾Ð¼ Ñ Ð¿Ð¾ÑÑÐ» Ð½Ð° ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÑ, Ð·Ð°ÑÐµÐ¼ Ñ ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÐ°Ð» Ð´Ð¾ Ð¾Ð±ÐµÐ´Ð°, Ð¿Ð¾ÑÐ»Ðµ ÑÑÐ¾Ð³Ð¾ Ñ Ð¿Ð¾Ð¾Ð±ÐµÐ´Ð°Ð», Ð¸ Ð½Ð°ÐºÐ¾Ð½ÐµÑ Ñ Ð²ÐµÑÐ½ÑÐ»ÑÑ Ð´Ð¾Ð¼Ð¾Ð¹. .Trim()

Notice that all verbs are in the past tense. The sequence words themselves don't change the verb form. So you just need to make sure each verb agrees with the subject in gender and number. If you are female, you say: Ð¡Ð½Ð°ÑÐ°Ð»Ð° Ñ Ð¿Ð¾Ð·Ð°Ð²ÑÑÐ°ÐºÐ°Ð»Ð°, Ð¿Ð¾ÑÐ¾Ð¼ Ñ Ð¿Ð¾ÑÐ»Ð° Ð½Ð° ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÑ, Ð·Ð°ÑÐµÐ¼ Ñ ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÐ°Ð»Ð° Ð´Ð¾ Ð¾Ð±ÐµÐ´Ð°, Ð¿Ð¾ÑÐ»Ðµ ÑÑÐ¾Ð³Ð¾ Ñ Ð¿Ð¾Ð¾Ð±ÐµÐ´Ð°Ð»Ð°, Ð¸ Ð½Ð°ÐºÐ¾Ð½ÐµÑ Ñ Ð²ÐµÑÐ½ÑÐ»Ð°ÑÑ Ð´Ð¾Ð¼Ð¾Ð¹. .Trim()

.Trim()

In African contexts, you might tell a story about your day as a trader: Ð¡Ð½Ð°ÑÐ°Ð»Ð° Ñ Ð¾ÑÐºÑÑÐ» Ð¼Ð°Ð³Ð°Ð·Ð¸Ð½ Ð² 7 ÑÑÑÐ°. ÐÐ¾ÑÐ¾Ð¼ Ð¿ÑÐ¸ÑÐ»Ð¸ Ð¿ÐµÑÐ²ÑÐµ Ð¿Ð¾ÐºÑÐ¿Ð°ÑÐµÐ»Ð¸. .Trim()

.Trim()

Aspects in the Past: Perfective vs. Imperfective

Russian verbs come in pairs: imperfective and perfective. The imperfective aspect describes ongoing, repeated, or habitual actions in the past. The perfective aspect describes a single, completed action with a result. For example, ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°ÑÑ (imperfective) means 'to read' in general, while Ð¿ÑÐ¾ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°ÑÑ (perfective) means 'to read and finish'. In the past tense, you choose the aspect based on what you want to say. .Trim()

.Trim()

Compare: ÐÑÐµÑÐ° Ñ ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°Ð» ÐºÐ½Ð¸Ð³Ñ. (Yesterday I was reading a book â imperfective, focusing on the process.) ÐÑÐµÑÐ° Ñ Ð¿ÑÐ¾ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°Ð» ÐºÐ½Ð¸Ð³Ñ. (Yesterday I read the book â perfective, focusing on completion.) The first sentence doesn't say if you finished; the second says you finished. Similarly, Ð¿Ð¸ÑÐ°ÑÑ (to write, imperfective) vs. Ð½Ð°Ð¿Ð¸ÑÐ°ÑÑ (to write, perfective): Ð¯ Ð¿Ð¸ÑÐ°Ð» Ð¿Ð¸ÑÑÐ¼Ð¾. (I was writing a letter.) Ð¯ Ð½Ð°Ð¿Ð¸ÑÐ°Ð» Ð¿Ð¸ÑÑÐ¼Ð¾. (I wrote the letter â and finished it.) .Trim()

.Trim()

In African business contexts, this distinction is crucial. If you say Ð¯ Ð¿ÑÐ¾Ð´Ð°Ð²Ð°Ð» ÑÐ¾Ð²Ð°ÑÑ, it means you were selling goods (maybe not all sold). If you say Ð¯ Ð¿ÑÐ¾Ð´Ð°Ð» ÑÐ¾Ð²Ð°ÑÑ, it means you sold the goods (all sold, completed). So, if you want to report success, use perfective: ÐÑÐµÑÐ° Ñ Ð¿ÑÐ¾Ð´Ð°Ð» 50 ÐºÐ¸Ð»Ð¾Ð³ÑÐ°Ð¼Ð¼Ð¾Ð² Ð¿Ð¾Ð¼Ð¸Ð´Ð¾ÑÐ¾Ð². (Yesterday I sold 50 kg of tomatoes.) If you just want to describe the activity, use imperfective: ÐÑÐµÑÐ° Ñ Ð¿ÑÐ¾Ð´Ð°Ð²Ð°Ð» Ð¿Ð¾Ð¼Ð¸Ð´Ð¾ÑÑ Ð²ÐµÑÑ Ð´ÐµÐ½Ñ. (Yesterday I was selling tomatoes all day.) .Trim()

.Trim()

Forming the perfective past is often regular: add the same gender/number endings to the perfective infinitive. For example, Ð¿ÑÐ¾ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°ÑÑ â Ð¿ÑÐ¾ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°Ð», Ð¿ÑÐ¾ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°Ð»Ð°, Ð¿ÑÐ¾ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°Ð»Ð¾, Ð¿ÑÐ¾ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°Ð»Ð¸. But note that some perfective verbs are irregular, like ÑÐºÐ°Ð·Ð°ÑÑ (to say, perfective) â ÑÐºÐ°Ð·Ð°Ð», ÑÐºÐ°Ð·Ð°Ð»Ð°, ÑÐºÐ°Ð·Ð°Ð»Ð¾, ÑÐºÐ°Ð·Ð°Ð»Ð¸. .Trim()

.Trim()

When narrating a sequence, you often use perfective for each completed step. For example: Ð¯ Ð²ÑÑÐ°Ð», ÑÐ¼ÑÐ»ÑÑ, Ð¿Ð¾Ð·Ð°Ð²ÑÑÐ°ÐºÐ°Ð» Ð¸ Ð¿Ð¾ÑÑÐ» Ð½Ð° ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÑ. (I got up, washed, had breakfast, and went to work.) All these are perfective because they are completed actions. But if you describe a background action, use imperfective: ÐÐ¾Ð³Ð´Ð° Ñ ÑÑÐ» Ð½Ð° ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÑ, Ñ Ð²ÑÑÑÐµÑÐ¸Ð» Ð´ÑÑÐ³Ð°. (When I was walking to work, I met a friend.) Here ÑÑÐ» is imperfective (ongoing), and Ð²ÑÑÑÐµÑÐ¸Ð» is perfective (completed). Practice by telling your daily routine using both aspects appropriately..Trim()

Module 10 · Future Tense and Plans

Future Tense: Simple and Compound

In Russian, the future tense has two forms: simple and compound. The simple future is used with perfective verbs. Perfective verbs describe actions that will be completed. To form the simple future, conjugate the perfective verb like a present tense verb. For example, 'Ñ Ð¿ÑÐ¾ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°Ñ' means 'I will read (and finish)'. The compound future uses the verb 'Ð±ÑÑÑ' (to be) in its future form plus the infinitive of an imperfective verb. The future forms of 'Ð±ÑÑÑ' are: Ñ Ð±ÑÐ´Ñ, ÑÑ Ð±ÑÐ´ÐµÑÑ, Ð¾Ð½/Ð¾Ð½Ð° Ð±ÑÐ´ÐµÑ, Ð¼Ñ Ð±ÑÐ´ÐµÐ¼, Ð²Ñ Ð±ÑÐ´ÐµÑÐµ, Ð¾Ð½Ð¸ Ð±ÑÐ´ÑÑ. Then add the imperfective infinitive. For example, 'Ñ Ð±ÑÐ´Ñ ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°ÑÑ' means 'I will be reading' or 'I will read' (without focusing on completion). Use the compound future for repeated or ongoing actions in the future. For example, 'ÐÐ°Ð¶Ð´ÑÐ¹ Ð´ÐµÐ½Ñ Ñ Ð±ÑÐ´Ñ ÑÑÐ¸ÑÑ ÑÑÑÑÐºÐ¸Ð¹ ÑÐ·ÑÐº' (Every day I will study Russian). In African business contexts, you might say 'ÐÐ°Ð²ÑÑÐ° Ñ Ð±ÑÐ´Ñ Ð·Ð²Ð¾Ð½Ð¸ÑÑ ÐºÐ»Ð¸ÐµÐ½ÑÑ' (Tomorrow I will call the client) using compound, or 'Ð¯ Ð¾ÑÐ¿ÑÐ°Ð²Ð»Ñ email' (I will send an email) using simple. Weather and Seasons

Talking about weather and seasons is essential for making plans. In Russian, the seasons are: Ð·Ð¸Ð¼Ð° (winter), Ð²ÐµÑÐ½Ð° (spring), Ð»ÐµÑÐ¾ (summer), Ð¾ÑÐµÐ½Ñ (autumn). Making Invitations and Suggestions

To invite someone in Russian, use 'ÐÐ°Ð²Ð°Ð¹' (informal) or 'ÐÐ°Ð²Ð°Ð¹ÑÐµ' (formal/plural) + verb in the future tense or infinitive. Conditional Sentences with ÐÑÐ»Ð¸

Conditional sentences with 'ÐµÑÐ»Ð¸' (if) are used to talk about real or possible conditions. The structure is: ÐÑÐ»Ð¸ + present tense, ... future tense. For example: 'ÐÑÐ»Ð¸ Ñ Ð¿Ð¾Ð»ÑÑÑ Ð³ÑÐ°Ð½Ñ, Ñ Ð¾ÑÐºÑÐ¾Ñ Ð±Ð¸Ð·Ð½ÐµÑ' (If I get a grant, I will open a business). Note that in Russian, after 'ÐµÑÐ»Ð¸', you use the present tense even though it refers to the future. So 'ÐµÑÐ»Ð¸ Ñ Ð¿Ð¾Ð»ÑÑÑ' literally means 'if I get' but implies future. The main clause uses the future tense. Another example: 'ÐÑÐ»Ð¸ Ð±ÑÐ´ÐµÑ Ð´Ð¾Ð¶Ð´Ñ, Ð¼Ñ Ð½Ðµ Ð¿Ð¾ÐµÐ´ÐµÐ¼ Ð½Ð° ÑÑÐ½Ð¾Ðº' (If it rains, we won't go to the market). For unreal conditions, use 'ÐµÑÐ»Ð¸ Ð±Ñ' + past tense, and the main clause with 'Ð±Ñ'. For example: 'ÐÑÐ»Ð¸ Ð±Ñ Ñ Ð¼ÐµÐ½Ñ Ð±ÑÐ»Ð¸ Ð´ÐµÐ½ÑÐ³Ð¸, Ñ Ð±Ñ ÐºÑÐ¿Ð¸Ð» Ð´Ð¾Ð¼' (If I had money, I would buy a house). In African business, you might say: 'ÐÑÐ»Ð¸ Ð¼Ñ ÑÐ½Ð¸Ð·Ð¸Ð¼ ÑÐµÐ½Ñ, Ð¼Ñ Ð¿ÑÐ¾Ð´Ð°Ð´Ð¸Ð¼ Ð±Ð¾Ð»ÑÑÐµ' (If we lower prices, we will sell more). Or 'ÐÑÐ»Ð¸ ÑÑ Ð±ÑÐ´ÐµÑÑ ÑÐ°Ð±Ð¾ÑÐ°ÑÑ ÑÑÐµÑÐ´Ð½Ð¾, ÑÑ Ð´Ð¾Ð±ÑÑÑÑÑÑ ÑÑÐ¿ÐµÑÐ°' (If you work hard, you will succeed). Remember that 'ÐµÑÐ»Ð¸' can also mean 'whether' in indirect questions: 'Ð¯ Ð½Ðµ Ð·Ð½Ð°Ñ, ÐµÑÐ»Ð¸ Ð¾Ð½ Ð¿ÑÐ¸Ð´ÑÑ' (I don't know if he will come). But more commonly, 'Ð»Ð¸' is used for 'whether'. Module 11 · Cases in Depth: Genitive, Dative, Instrumental

Genitive Case: Possession and Quantity

The genitive case is the most common case in Russian. It answers the questions 'of what?' or 'of whom?' and is used to show possession, absence, or quantity. For example, 'ÐºÐ½Ð¸Ð³Ð° Ð±ÑÐ°ÑÐ°' means 'brother's book' (literally 'book of brother'). In African business contexts, you might say 'Ð´Ð¸ÑÐµÐºÑÐ¾Ñ ÐºÐ¾Ð¼Ð¿Ð°Ð½Ð¸Ð¸' (director of the company) or 'ÑÐµÐ½Ð° ÑÐ¾Ð²Ð°ÑÐ°' (price of the goods). For feminine nouns ending in -Ð°, change to -Ñ (e.g., ÑÐµÑÑÑÐ° â ÑÐµÑÑÑÑ). For neuter nouns ending in -Ð¾, change to -Ð° (e.g., Ð¾ÐºÐ½Ð¾ â Ð¾ÐºÐ½Ð°). Plural forms are more complex but follow patterns: masculine and neuter take -Ð¾Ð²/-ÐµÐ², feminine take -Ã¸ (no ending) or -ÐµÐ¹..Trim()

.Trim()

Quantity expressions also require the genitive. After numbers like 2, 3, 4, use the genitive singular: 'Ð´Ð²Ð° Ð±ÑÐ°ÑÐ°' (two brothers), 'ÑÑÐ¸ ÑÐµÑÑÑÑ' (three sisters). The dative case answers 'to whom?' or 'for whom?' and is used for indirect objects, as well as with certain verbs and expressions of feeling. In real Russian sentences, multiple cases often appear together. For example, 'Ð¯ Ð´Ð°Ñ ÐºÐ½Ð¸Ð³Ñ Ð±ÑÐ°ÑÑ' (I give the book to my brother) â 'ÐºÐ½Ð¸Ð³Ñ' is accusative, 'Ð±ÑÐ°ÑÑ' is dative. .Trim()

Module 12 · Russian Culture and Advanced Conversation

Russian Holidays and Traditions

Russians celebrate many holidays, but the most important are New Year (Novy God) and Victory Day (Den Pobedy). New Year is like Christmas in the West: families gather, decorate a fir tree (yolka), and exchange gifts. Ded Moroz (Father Frost) and Snegurochka (Snow Maiden) bring presents. The holiday lasts from December 31 to January 1, with the president's address at midnight. Victory Day on May 9 commemorates the end of World War II in Europe. Parades, fireworks, and the Immortal Regiment march honor veterans. In Africa, you can join Russian expat communities in cities like Nairobi or Lagos to experience these traditions. For example, the Russian Embassy in Kenya often hosts a New Year party. To practice, learn key phrases: 'S Novym Godom!' (Happy New Year!) and 'S Dnyom Pobedy!' (Happy Victory Day!). Maslenitsa is a week-long pancake festival before Lent. It marks the end of winter. People eat bliny (pancakes) with butter, caviar, or jam, and burn a scarecrow. In Africa, you can make bliny using local ingredients like cassava flour. Another important holiday is International Women's Day on March 8, when men give flowers and gifts to women. This is widely celebrated in Russia and many African countries too. For business, knowing these holidays helps you schedule meetings. Russians take long holidays in early January and early May. Avoid scheduling calls then. Also, if invited to a Russian home for a holiday, bring a gift like chocolate or flowers (odd number, not red). To deepen your learning, watch Russian holiday videos on YouTube and repeat the greetings. Practice with a language partner on apps like HelloTalk. Set a goal: learn 10 holiday-related words per week. By the end of this lesson, you should be able to name three major Russian holidays and greet someone appropriately on each..Trim()

Idioms and Common Expressions

Idioms make you sound fluent. Russian has thousands, but start with 20 common ones. For example, 'ÐÐ¸ Ð¿ÑÑÐ°, Ð½Ð¸ Ð¿ÐµÑÐ°' (Ni puha, ni pera) literally means 'Neither fluff nor feather' but is used to wish someone good luck, like 'Break a leg!' The response is 'Ð ÑÑÑÑÑ!' (K chyortu!) meaning 'To the devil!' Another: 'ÐÐ¸ÑÑ Ð±Ð°ÐºÐ»ÑÑÐ¸' (Bit' baklushi) means to goof off. 'ÐÐ¾Ð´Ð¸ÑÑ Ð·Ð° Ð½Ð¾Ñ' (Vodit' za nos) means to lead someone on. 'ÐÐ°Ðº ÑÑÐ±Ð° Ð² Ð²Ð¾Ð´Ðµ' (Kak ryba v vode) means 'like a fish in water' â to be in one's element. In African business contexts, using idioms shows cultural understanding. For instance, when negotiating, you might say 'ÐÑ Ð½Ðµ Ð² Ð»ÐµÑÑ' (My ne v lesu) meaning 'We are not in the forest' â i.e., we are civilized, let's be reasonable. Or 'Ð¢Ð¸ÑÐµ ÐµÐ´ÐµÑÑ â Ð´Ð°Ð»ÑÑÐµ Ð±ÑÐ´ÐµÑÑ' (Tishe edesh â dalshe budesh) meaning 'The quieter you go, the further you'll get' â patience pays. To learn idioms, use flashcards with Anki. Create 5 cards per day. Practice with a Russian speaker on iTalki. Listen to Russian podcasts like 'Russian Pod 101' for context. Write a short dialogue using three idioms. For example: A: 'ÐÐ°Ðº Ð´ÐµÐ»Ð°?' B: 'ÐÐ°Ðº ÑÑÐ±Ð° Ð² Ð²Ð¾Ð´Ðµ!' A: 'ÐÐ¸ Ð¿ÑÑÐ°, Ð½Ð¸ Ð¿ÐµÑÐ° Ð½Ð° ÑÐºÐ·Ð°Ð¼ÐµÐ½Ðµ!' B: 'Ð ÑÑÑÑÑ!' Record yourself and compare with native audio. In Africa, you can join Russian language clubs on Telegram. Search for 'Russian language Africa' to find partners. Also, watch Russian movies with subtitles to hear idioms in action. 'ÐÑÐ¾Ð½Ð¸Ñ ÑÑÐ´ÑÐ±Ñ' (Irony of Fate) is a classic New Year film full of expressions. Aim to master 5 idioms per week. By module end, you'll have 30. Remember, idioms are fixed â don't change words. Use them sparingly in formal writing, but freely in conversation..Trim()

Discussing Art, Music, and Literature

Russian culture has global giants: Tolstoy, Dostoevsky, Chekhov in literature; Tchaikovsky, Rachmaninoff, Stravinsky in music; Repin, Kandinsky, Malevich in art. To discuss them, learn key vocabulary: 'ÑÑÐ´Ð¾Ð¶Ð½Ð¸Ðº' (artist), 'ÐºÐ¾Ð¼Ð¿Ð¾Ð·Ð¸ÑÐ¾Ñ' (composer), 'Ð¿Ð¸ÑÐ°ÑÐµÐ»Ñ' (writer), 'ÐºÐ°ÑÑÐ¸Ð½Ð°' (painting), 'ÑÐ¾Ð¼Ð°Ð½' (novel), 'ÑÐ¸Ð¼ÑÐ¾Ð½Ð¸Ñ' (symphony). Start with simple sentences: 'Ð¯ Ð»ÑÐ±Ð»Ñ Ð§ÐµÑÐ¾Ð²Ð°' (I love Chekhov). 'ÐÐ½Ðµ Ð½ÑÐ°Ð²Ð¸ÑÑÑ ÐºÐ°ÑÑÐ¸Ð½Ð° Â«Ð§ÑÑÐ½ÑÐ¹ ÐºÐ²Ð°Ð´ÑÐ°ÑÂ»' (I like the painting 'Black Square'). For African learners, connect to local art. Compare Russian folk art (khokhloma, gzhel) with African patterns. Discuss how both use bright colors and nature motifs. In music, Russian folk songs use balalaika and accordion; African music uses kora and djembe. You can say: 'Ð ÑÑÑÐºÐ°Ñ Ð½Ð°ÑÐ¾Ð´Ð½Ð°Ñ Ð¼ÑÐ·ÑÐºÐ° Ð¿Ð¾ÑÐ¾Ð¶Ð° Ð½Ð° Ð°ÑÑÐ¸ÐºÐ°Ð½ÑÐºÑÑ' (Russian folk music is similar to African). For literature, read short stories by Chekhov in translation, then summarize in Russian. Use tools like Forvo for pronunciation of author names. Join a book club on Goodreads or Discord. In Africa, the Russian Centre for Science and Culture in Addis Ababa, Cairo, or Nairobi often hosts art exhibitions and film screenings. Attend and practice speaking. Write a 5-sentence review of a Russian artwork. Example: 'ÐÐ½Ðµ Ð½ÑÐ°Ð²Ð¸ÑÑÑ ÐºÐ°ÑÑÐ¸Ð½Ð° Â«Ð£ÑÑÐ¾ Ð² ÑÐ¾ÑÐ½Ð¾Ð²Ð¾Ð¼ Ð»ÐµÑÑÂ» Ð¨Ð¸ÑÐºÐ¸Ð½Ð°. ÐÐ° ÐºÐ°ÑÑÐ¸Ð½Ðµ Ð¼ÐµÐ´Ð²ÐµÐ´Ð¸ Ð² Ð»ÐµÑÑ. Ð­ÑÐ¾ ÑÐµÐ°Ð»Ð¸Ð·Ð¼. Ð¯ ÑÑÐ²ÑÑÐ²ÑÑ ÑÐ¿Ð¾ÐºÐ¾Ð¹ÑÑÐ²Ð¸Ðµ.' (I like Shishkin's painting 'Morning in a Pine Forest'. Current Events and Opinions

To discuss current events, you need vocabulary for news: 'Ð½Ð¾Ð²Ð¾ÑÑÐ¸' (news), 'Ð¿Ð¾Ð»Ð¸ÑÐ¸ÐºÐ°' (politics), 'ÑÐºÐ¾Ð½Ð¾Ð¼Ð¸ÐºÐ°' (economy), 'ÑÐ¾Ð±ÑÑÐ¸Ðµ' (event), 'Ð¼Ð½ÐµÐ½Ð¸Ðµ' (opinion). Start by reading simplified news on 'Russian Today' or 'Meduza' (some have English). For Africa-focused news, follow 'African News' in Russian on TASS or Sputnik. Practice expressing opinions: 'Ð¯ Ð´ÑÐ¼Ð°Ñ, ÑÑÐ¾...' (I think that...), 'ÐÐ¾ Ð¼Ð¾ÐµÐ¼Ñ Ð¼Ð½ÐµÐ½Ð¸Ñ...' (In my opinion...), 'Ð¯ ÑÐ¾Ð³Ð»Ð°ÑÐµÐ½/Ð½Ðµ ÑÐ¾Ð³Ð»Ð°ÑÐµÐ½' (I agree/disagree). For example: 'Ð¯ Ð´ÑÐ¼Ð°Ñ, ÑÑÐ¾ ÑÐºÐ¾Ð½Ð¾Ð¼Ð¸ÐºÐ° Ð² ÐÑÑÐ¸ÐºÐµ ÑÐ°ÑÑÑÑ' (I think the economy in Africa is growing). 'ÐÐ¾ Ð¼Ð¾ÐµÐ¼Ñ Ð¼Ð½ÐµÐ½Ð¸Ñ, Ð Ð¾ÑÑÐ¸Ñ Ð¸ ÐÑÑÐ¸ÐºÐ° Ð´Ð¾Ð»Ð¶Ð½Ñ ÑÐ¾ÑÑÑÐ´Ð½Ð¸ÑÐ°ÑÑ' (In my opinion, Russia and Africa should cooperate). Use connectors: 'Ð¾Ð´Ð½Ð°ÐºÐ¾' (however), 'Ð¿Ð¾ÑÑÐ¾Ð¼Ñ' (therefore), 'ÐºÑÐ¾Ð¼Ðµ ÑÐ¾Ð³Ð¾' (besides). To stay informed, listen to 'Russian News' podcasts. Set a daily goal: read one headline, translate it, and form an opinion. Write a short paragraph: 'Ð¡ÐµÐ³Ð¾Ð´Ð½Ñ Ñ Ð¿ÑÐ¾ÑÐ¸ÑÐ°Ð», ÑÑÐ¾ Ð Ð¾ÑÑÐ¸Ñ Ð¾ÑÐ¿ÑÐ°Ð²Ð»ÑÐµÑ Ð·ÐµÑÐ½Ð¾ Ð² ÐÑÑÐ¸ÐºÑ. Ð¯ Ð´ÑÐ¼Ð°Ñ, ÑÑÐ¾ ÑÐ¾ÑÐ¾ÑÐ¾, Ð¿Ð¾ÑÐ¾Ð¼Ñ ÑÑÐ¾...' (Today I read that Russia sends grain to Africa. I think it's good because...). In Africa, you can discuss with friends in Russian language meetups. Use apps like Tandem to find Russian speakers interested in African affairs. For formal debates, learn phrases: 'Ð¡ Ð¾Ð´Ð½Ð¾Ð¹ ÑÑÐ¾ÑÐ¾Ð½Ñ... Ñ Ð´ÑÑÐ³Ð¾Ð¹ ÑÑÐ¾ÑÐ¾Ð½Ñ...' (On one hand... on the other hand...). Avoid sensitive topics like politics initially; focus on culture, sports, weather. Practice with news articles from 'Kommersant' or 'RBK'. Use Google Translate for difficult words, but try to guess from context. Aim to learn 15 news-related words per week. By the end, you can give a 1-minute opinion on a current event..Trim()

Formal vs. Informal Writing Styles

Russian has distinct formal (Ð²Ñ) and informal (ÑÑ) styles. Use 'Ð²Ñ' with strangers, elders, officials, and in business. Use 'ÑÑ' with friends, family, children, and peers. In writing, formal letters start with 'Ð£Ð²Ð°Ð¶Ð°ÐµÐ¼ÑÐ¹ ÐÐ²Ð°Ð½ ÐÐ²Ð°Ð½Ð¾Ð²Ð¸Ñ!' (Dear Ivan Ivanovich!) and end with 'Ð¡ ÑÐ²Ð°Ð¶ÐµÐ½Ð¸ÐµÐ¼, ...' (Sincerely, ...). Informal emails start with 'ÐÑÐ¸Ð²ÐµÑ, ÐÐ²Ð°Ð½!' and end with 'ÐÐ¾ÐºÐ°, ...' or 'ÐÐ±Ð½Ð¸Ð¼Ð°Ñ, ...' (Hugs, ...). For business in Africa, formal Russian is crucial. When emailing a Russian company, use full name and patronymic. Example: 'Ð£Ð²Ð°Ð¶Ð°ÐµÐ¼Ð°Ñ ÐÐ½Ð½Ð° ÐÐµÑÑÐ¾Ð²Ð½Ð°! Ð¯ Ð¿Ð¸ÑÑ Ð²Ð°Ð¼ Ð¿Ð¾ Ð¿Ð¾Ð²Ð¾Ð´Ñ ÑÐ¾ÑÑÑÐ´Ð½Ð¸ÑÐµÑÑÐ²Ð°...' (Dear Anna Petrovna! I am writing to you regarding cooperation...). Avoid contractions and slang. Use 'Ð¿Ð¾Ð¶Ð°Ð»ÑÐ¹ÑÑÐ°' (please) and 'Ð±ÑÐ´ÑÑÐµ Ð´Ð¾Ð±ÑÑ' (be so kind). For informal writing, like social media, use 'ÑÑ', emojis, and abbreviations: 'ÑÐ¿Ñ' (thanks), 'Ð½Ð¾ÑÐ¼' (okay). Practice by writing two versions of the same message. Formal: 'ÐÐ´ÑÐ°Ð²ÑÑÐ²ÑÐ¹ÑÐµ! ÐÐµ Ð¼Ð¾Ð³Ð»Ð¸ Ð±Ñ Ð²Ñ Ð¾ÑÐ¿ÑÐ°Ð²Ð¸ÑÑ Ð¼Ð½Ðµ Ð´Ð¾ÐºÑÐ¼ÐµÐ½ÑÑ?' (Hello! Could you send me the documents?) Informal: 'ÐÑÐ¸Ð²ÐµÑ! Ð¡ÐºÐ¸Ð½Ñ Ð´Ð¾ÐºÑÐ¼ÐµÐ½ÑÑ, Ð¿Ð»Ð¸Ð·.' (Hi! Send the docs, please.) Use tools like 'Grammarly' for Russian (not available, but use 'Ð¯Ð½Ð´ÐµÐºÑ.Ð¡Ð¿ÐµÐ»Ð»ÐµÑ'). In Africa, when applying for jobs in Russian companies, use formal style. Write a cover letter in Russian: 'Ð£Ð²Ð°Ð¶Ð°ÐµÐ¼ÑÐ¹ Ð¾ÑÐ´ÐµÐ» ÐºÐ°Ð´ÑÐ¾Ð²! ÐÑÐ¾ÑÑ ÑÐ°ÑÑÐ¼Ð¾ÑÑÐµÑÑ Ð¼Ð¾Ñ ÐºÐ°Ð½Ð´Ð¸Ð´Ð°ÑÑÑÑ...' (Dear HR department! Please consider my application...). For informal practice, text a Russian friend on WhatsApp. Learn to switch: if someone uses 'Ð²Ñ', respond with 'Ð²Ñ'; if 'ÑÑ', use 'ÑÑ'. Aim to write one formal and one informal message per day. By the end, you can write a professional email and a casual note..Trim()

Final Project: Presentation in Russian

Your final project is a 5-minute presentation in Russian on a topic of your choice, connecting Russian culture to Africa. Choose a topic like 'Russian holidays in Africa', 'Comparing Russian and African art', or 'How to do business with Russians'. Structure: introduction, 3 main points, conclusion. Write a script of 300-400 words. Use vocabulary from all lessons. Practice with a timer. Record yourself and listen for mistakes. Use tools: 'Audacity' for recording, 'Forvo' for pronunciation. Get feedback from a tutor on iTalki or a language partner on HelloTalk. In Africa, present to your class or a local Russian club. For example, the Russian Embassy in Nairobi might host a cultural evening. Prepare slides with images. Speak slowly and clearly. Use gestures. Example script: 'ÐÐ´ÑÐ°Ð²ÑÑÐ²ÑÐ¹ÑÐµ! Ð¡ÐµÐ³Ð¾Ð´Ð½Ñ Ñ ÑÐ°ÑÑÐºÐ°Ð¶Ñ Ð¾ ÑÑÑÑÐºÐ¸Ñ Ð¿ÑÐ°Ð·Ð´Ð½Ð¸ÐºÐ°Ñ Ð² ÐÑÑÐ¸ÐºÐµ. Ð ÐÐµÐ½Ð¸Ð¸ Ð¼Ñ Ð¿ÑÐ°Ð·Ð´Ð½ÑÐµÐ¼ ÐÐ¾Ð²ÑÐ¹ Ð³Ð¾Ð´ Ñ ÑÑÑÑÐºÐ¸Ð¼Ð¸ Ð´ÑÑÐ·ÑÑÐ¼Ð¸. ÐÑ ÐµÐ´Ð¸Ð¼ Ð±Ð»Ð¸Ð½Ñ Ð¸ Ð¿Ð¾ÑÐ¼ Ð¿ÐµÑÐ½Ð¸. Ð­ÑÐ¾ Ð¾Ð±ÑÐµÐ´Ð¸Ð½ÑÐµÑ ÐºÑÐ»ÑÑÑÑÑ.' (Hello! Today I will talk about Russian holidays in Africa. In Kenya we celebrate New Year with Russian friends. We eat pancakes and sing songs. It unites cultures.) Practice 10 times. Memorize key phrases, but don't read. Make eye contact. After presentation, answer 3 questions from the audience. This proves advanced conversation. Submit a video recording to rise AFRICA skills for certification. Tips: use a quiet room, good lighting. Speak at 120 words per minute. Pause at commas. Emphasize important words. If you forget a word, use a synonym or describe it. For example, instead of 'Ð±Ð°Ð»Ð°Ð»Ð°Ð¹ÐºÐ°', say 'ÑÑÑÑÐºÐ¸Ð¹ Ð¼ÑÐ·ÑÐºÐ°Ð»ÑÐ½ÑÐ¹ Ð¸Ð½ÑÑÑÑÐ¼ÐµÐ½Ñ' (Russian musical instrument). Aim to impress with cultural insights. This project prepares you for real-life presentations in Russian, whether in business, education, or diplomacy. Good luck!.Trim()
